========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly at work. I mean, my friends are patient with me, and my husband is too, but in the shop it’s started to matter. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I’ll lose the thread during client consultations—like they’re asking about a setting and I’m suddenly thinking about whether I ordered the little velvet boxes, which is not the point.

And repairs… I’ve missed deadlines, not huge disasters, but enough that I feel awful. My husband has been checking invoices for me now, which is embarrassing because I used to handle all of that myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.

And repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.

My husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.

It’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.

Like at work, someone asks me something simple, you know, “When will this ring repair be ready?” and I start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers, and I can see their face kind of… they’re losing me. I don’t mean to do it. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with answering questions straight affecting your relationships at work or with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I mean, my friends are patient with me, and my husband is too, but in the shop it’s started to matter. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I’ll lose the thread during client consultations—like they’re asking about a setting and I’m suddenly thinking about whether I ordered the little velvet boxes, which is not the point.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines, not huge disasters, but enough that I feel awful. My husband has been checking invoices for me now, which is embarrassing because I used to handle all of that myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty focusing or paying attention to tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I start trying to answer or stay with what someone’s saying, and then my mind grabs something else. Like with a customer asking when a ring repair will be ready—I’ll mean to answer that, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, or the mortgage papers, and I can see on their face that I’ve lost them. I don’t mean to wander off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've lost track of the conversation or task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I get upset. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. I’ll hear myself doing it—someone asks, “When will the ring be ready?” and I’m supposed to just say Tuesday or Friday, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and that reminds me of the garden soil delivery, and then somehow the mortgage papers sitting on the counter at home.\n\nAnd I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t. That’s when I feel this kind of sinking feeling, like, oh no, I’ve done it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or physical illnesses recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not a head injury. I haven’t hit my head or anything like that recently.\n\nPhysical illness… I mean, I get the usual tired or run-down feeling sometimes, but nothing that seems like an illness. I did have this whole thing where I thought maybe it was the lighting in the shop or the fumes from the polishing compounds, because sometimes when you’re working with tiny pieces you start blaming the lamp or the bench setup… but no, I don’t think I’ve been sick in any real way recently.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: I’m 53… though I have to think about it for a second sometimes, because after 50 the numbers all feel like they started sliding together. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.

Like at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.

That’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.

I’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.

And repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.

description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: My husband says I move slower at the bench now, like even picking up tweezers takes effort, though when I’m anxious I’ll rub my fingers together until they’re sore.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a quiet day, my body feels worn out as if I spent hours hauling display cases, and I have to push myself to water the garden or answer emails.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or manage your jewelry shop compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.\n\nI’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.

And then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.4, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… it’s been hard to answer people straight. Like at work, someone asks me something simple, you know, “When will this ring repair be ready?” and I start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers, and I can see their face kind of… they’re losing me. I don’t mean to do it. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with answering questions straight affecting your relationships at work or with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I mean, my friends are patient with me, and my husband is too, but in the shop it’s started to matter. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I’ll lose the thread during client consultations—like they’re asking about a setting and I’m suddenly thinking about whether I ordered the little velvet boxes, which is not the point.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines, not huge disasters, but enough that I feel awful. My husband has been checking invoices for me now, which is embarrassing because I used to handle all of that myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty focusing or paying attention to tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I start trying to answer or stay with what someone’s saying, and then my mind grabs something else. Like with a customer asking when a ring repair will be ready—I’ll mean to answer that, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, or the mortgage papers, and I can see on their face that I’ve lost them. I don’t mean to wander off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've lost track of the conversation or task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I get upset. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. I’ll hear myself doing it—someone asks, “When will the ring be ready?” and I’m supposed to just say Tuesday or Friday, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and that reminds me of the garden soil delivery, and then somehow the mortgage papers sitting on the counter at home.\n\nAnd I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t. That’s when I feel this kind of sinking feeling, like, oh no, I’ve done it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or physical illnesses recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not a head injury. I haven’t hit my head or anything like that recently.\n\nPhysical illness… I mean, I get the usual tired or run-down feeling sometimes, but nothing that seems like an illness. I did have this whole thing where I thought maybe it was the lighting in the shop or the fumes from the polishing compounds, because sometimes when you’re working with tiny pieces you start blaming the lamp or the bench setup… but no, I don’t think I’ve been sick in any real way recently.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: I’m 53… though I have to think about it for a second sometimes, because after 50 the numbers all feel like they started sliding together. But yes, 53.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with staying on topic and answering questions straight start suddenly or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it to a sudden start. It’s more like… I noticed it because it started happening in conversations at work. Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.\n\nLike at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.

Someone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.

So yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.

I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.

As far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.

So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.

Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.\n\nLike at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or manage your jewelry shop compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.\n\nI’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.\n\nSomeone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to remember and recall information over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.\n\nAs far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.\n\nSomeone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to remember and recall information over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.\n\nAs far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your main problem is that you can't focus on what you're doing or that you can't remember what you need to do?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"I came in because when people ask me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready, my answer starts going from invoices to garden soil to the mortgage papers and I can see them losing me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I can’t keep hold of what I’m doing while I’m speaking, maybe… not exactly forgetting, like it’s erased.

That’s partly why I came in. Someone asks me something simple at the shop—when a ring repair will be ready, say—and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil comes into my head, and then the mortgage papers, and I can see their face, like they’re trying to follow me and I’ve gone off somewhere else.

So… focus, I suppose. Or keeping the answer on the track. The remembering is there too, but that drifting when I’m trying to answer is what worries me more.

I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. Like at work, someone asks me something simple, you know, “When will this ring repair be ready?” and I start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers, and I can see their face kind of… they’re losing me. My husband has been checking invoices for me now, which is embarrassing because I used to handle all of that myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty focusing or paying attention to tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I start trying to answer or stay with what someone’s saying, and then my mind grabs something else. I don’t mean to wander off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've lost track of the conversation or task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I get upset. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. That’s when I feel this kind of sinking feeling, like, oh no, I’ve done it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or physical illnesses recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not a head injury. But yes, 53.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with staying on topic and answering questions straight start suddenly or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it to a sudden start. It’s more like… I noticed it because it started happening in conversations at work. Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I’ll lose the thread during client consultations—like they’re asking about a setting and I’m suddenly thinking about whether I ordered the little velvet boxes, which is not the point.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines, not huge disasters, but enough that I feel awful. My husband has been checking invoices for me now, which is embarrassing because I used to handle all of that myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty focusing or paying attention to tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I start trying to answer or stay with what someone’s saying, and then my mind grabs something else. Like with a customer asking when a ring repair will be ready—I’ll mean to answer that, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, or the mortgage papers, and I can see on their face that I’ve lost them. I don’t mean to wander off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've lost track of the conversation or task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I get upset. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. I’ll hear myself doing it—someone asks, “When will the ring be ready?” and I’m supposed to just say Tuesday or Friday, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and that reminds me of the garden soil delivery, and then somehow the mortgage papers sitting on the counter at home.\n\nAnd I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t. That’s when I feel this kind of sinking feeling, like, oh no, I’ve done it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or physical illnesses recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not a head injury. I haven’t hit my head or anything like that recently.\n\nPhysical illness… I mean, I get the usual tired or run-down feeling sometimes, but nothing that seems like an illness. I did have this whole thing where I thought maybe it was the lighting in the shop or the fumes from the polishing compounds, because sometimes when you’re working with tiny pieces you start blaming the lamp or the bench setup… but no, I don’t think I’ve been sick in any real way recently.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: I’m 53… though I have to think about it for a second sometimes, because after 50 the numbers all feel like they started sliding together. But yes, 53.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with staying on topic and answering questions straight start suddenly or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it to a sudden start. It’s more like… I noticed it because it started happening in conversations at work. Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.\n\nLike at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or manage your jewelry shop compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.\n\nI’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.\n\nSomeone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to remember and recall information over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.\n\nAs far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus on what you're doing or that you can't remember what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t keep hold of what I’m doing while I’m speaking, maybe… not exactly forgetting, like it’s erased.\n\nThat’s partly why I came in. Someone asks me something simple at the shop—when a ring repair will be ready, say—and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil comes into my head, and then the mortgage papers, and I can see their face, like they’re trying to follow me and I’ve gone off somewhere else.\n\nSo… focus, I suppose. Or keeping the answer on the track. The remembering is there too, but that drifting when I’m trying to answer is what worries me more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders off because you're not interested in what you're doing, or because you're having trouble keeping your thoughts organized?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because I’m not interested. I mean, at the shop I am trying to answer them, and I care about getting it right.\n\nIt feels more like I can’t keep the thoughts in order. Someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start there, but then I’m on the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the garden soil by the shed, and then mortgage papers somehow come into it. And I can see the person’s face like… they’re losing the thread because I lost it first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll hear myself doing it—someone asks, “When will the ring be ready?” and I’m supposed to just say Tuesday or Friday, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and that reminds me of the garden soil delivery, and then somehow the mortgage papers sitting on the counter at home.\n\nAnd I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t. That’s when I feel this kind of sinking feeling, like, oh no, I’ve done it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any head injuries or physical illnesses recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not a head injury. I haven’t hit my head or anything like that recently.\n\nPhysical illness… I mean, I get the usual tired or run-down feeling sometimes, but nothing that seems like an illness. I did have this whole thing where I thought maybe it was the lighting in the shop or the fumes from the polishing compounds, because sometimes when you’re working with tiny pieces you start blaming the lamp or the bench setup… but no, I don’t think I’ve been sick in any real way recently.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: I’m 53… though I have to think about it for a second sometimes, because after 50 the numbers all feel like they started sliding together. But yes, 53.\n\nDoctor: Did these difficulties with staying on topic and answering questions straight start suddenly or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it to a sudden start. It’s more like… I noticed it because it started happening in conversations at work. Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.\n\nLike at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or manage your jewelry shop compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.\n\nI’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.\n\nSomeone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to remember and recall information over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.\n\nAs far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus on what you're doing or that you can't remember what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t keep hold of what I’m doing while I’m speaking, maybe… not exactly forgetting, like it’s erased.\n\nThat’s partly why I came in. Someone asks me something simple at the shop—when a ring repair will be ready, say—and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil comes into my head, and then the mortgage papers, and I can see their face, like they’re trying to follow me and I’ve gone off somewhere else.\n\nSo… focus, I suppose. Or keeping the answer on the track. The remembering is there too, but that drifting when I’m trying to answer is what worries me more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders off because you're not interested in what you're doing, or because you're having trouble keeping your thoughts organized?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because I’m not interested. I mean, at the shop I am trying to answer them, and I care about getting it right.\n\nIt feels more like I can’t keep the thoughts in order. Someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start there, but then I’m on the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the garden soil by the shed, and then mortgage papers somehow come into it. And I can see the person’s face like… they’re losing the thread because I lost it first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on topic is related to a change in your thinking or memory, or is it more about being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like my thinking gets… tangled, not just distracted. I came in because people ask me a simple thing, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer that, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil is in my head, and then the mortgage papers.\n\nAnd I can see their face, like I’ve lost them. Or I’ve lost myself first, maybe. It’s not that I don’t care about the answer. It just doesn’t stay in the right lane.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is affecting things more, or at least it’s the part that makes trouble in the day.

The sadness is there, and I get embarrassed—especially when a customer is looking at me and I know I’m not making sense—but the staying on topic part is what’s made me cut back on custom work. I used to handle those conversations fine, all the details about settings and sizes and dates, and now I lose track during consultations.

And then repairs… I miss deadlines because I think I’ve said one thing or written it down, and then the invoice—my husband has been checking those for me now. I used to do that myself. It’s humiliating, but also practical, because if the numbers are wrong, or the date is wrong, then the whole thing gets messy.

I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. It’s more like… I noticed it because it started happening in conversations at work. Someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and instead of just answering, I’m off talking about the invoice, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers at home.\n\nSo I don’t know exactly when it began, but it became obvious when people started looking confused and I realized I wasn’t answering the question straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory or ability to learn new things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big change like that. I mean, I forget where I put a tool sometimes, but in jewelry work there are so many tiny things—stones, clasps, little bags with labels—and if the bench is messy, anyone would lose track.\n\nLearning new things… I haven’t really been trying to learn a whole new trade or anything. If someone shows me a new way to set something, I can usually follow it, though I may need to write it down. But I’ve always liked writing things down. So… no, I’m not sure I’d call it a change over the past few years.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or work-related tasks because of your difficulty staying on topic or answering questions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have, in a practical way. I’ve cut back on custom work, because those appointments need you to really stay with the customer—what stone they want, the setting, the budget—and sometimes I lose the thread halfway through and then I’m embarrassed.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines I shouldn’t miss. It’s not like I don’t know how to do the work. I do. But the conversations and the notes and the timing all get tangled, and then suddenly Mrs. Harper’s ring—well, not Mrs. Harper maybe, but someone’s ring—isn’t ready when I said it would be.\n\nMy husband has been double-checking invoices now, which I used to handle myself. I don’t love that. It makes me feel a little… reduced, I suppose. But it helps, because numbers and notes can wander on me if I’m also trying to talk to a client.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you, such as noises or movements?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s noises exactly. I mean, the shop has noises all day—the polishing wheel, people coming in, the little bell on the door—and I can usually work around that.\n\nIt’s more when someone asks me a simple question, like when a ring repair will be ready. I’ll start answering, and then somehow I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil, and then the mortgage papers at home… and I can see their face change, like I’ve lost them. So maybe I’m distracted, but it feels more like my answer just wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to focus and stay on topic has gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s not that I can’t do the jewelry work, exactly. It’s when somebody asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, because I was thinking about the bags by the shed, and then the mortgage papers at home…\n\nAnd I can see the person’s face, you know, like they’re waiting for me to come back to the question. So yes, I think staying on topic has gotten worse. Not all the time, but enough that it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself using words or phrases that don't make sense to others, or having trouble finding the right words to express what you're trying to say?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not so much made-up words, I don’t think… but I’ll be trying to answer plainly and it just comes out wrong, or it goes somewhere else.\n\nLike at the shop, if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair, then I’m talking about the invoice, and then garden soil because I remembered the bags by the shed, and then somehow the mortgage papers at home. And I can see them sort of… waiting for me to come back.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I mean, but it doesn’t always arrive that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or manage your jewelry shop compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still open the shop and I can still do the basic repairs, but it’s not like it was a few years ago.\n\nI’ve had to cut back on the custom pieces, which I hate, because that was the part I used to really take pride in—the little sketches, choosing stones, talking through what someone wanted. But now during consultations I’ll lose track of what they asked for, or I’ll write something down and later it doesn’t quite match what I remember.\n\nAnd repairs… I’ve missed deadlines. Not huge disasters, but enough that people have called, and then I feel awful. My husband double-checks the invoices now, too. I used to do all that myself without thinking. He’s kind about it, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite running my own shop the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice little things, like the light on the display cases, or a stone that should look pretty, and it just… doesn’t reach me the same way. I start thinking about whether I left the kettle on, or if the back door sticks when it rains—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, my mood has been lower. More mornings than not, it feels heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with remembering familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… familiar words, names, sometimes. It’s less like they vanish completely and more like I’m reaching for them and grab the wrong thread.\n\nSomeone will ask me something simple at the counter, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I mean to just answer that. But then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I remember the bags of garden soil by the shed, and somehow I’ve ended up mentioning the mortgage papers at home. And I can see their face change, like they’re trying to follow me but I’ve lost them.\n\nSo yes, I do have trouble with words sometimes… or with keeping the words in the right lane, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to remember and recall information over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I mean, I do misplace things sometimes, but doesn’t everyone? Keys, receipts, those little repair envelopes if I don’t put them in the same spot… though the envelopes are annoying because they’re all the same color unless I mark them.\n\nAs far as remembering information… I can usually recall things if I’ve written them down, or if someone gives me a minute. Sometimes I get flustered and then it slips away, like when a customer is waiting and the phone rings, and then I’m thinking about whether the clasp drawer got reorganized—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some small things, but I can’t say for sure that there’s been a big decline over the years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus on what you're doing or that you can't remember what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t keep hold of what I’m doing while I’m speaking, maybe… not exactly forgetting, like it’s erased.\n\nThat’s partly why I came in. Someone asks me something simple at the shop—when a ring repair will be ready, say—and I start to answer, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil comes into my head, and then the mortgage papers, and I can see their face, like they’re trying to follow me and I’ve gone off somewhere else.\n\nSo… focus, I suppose. Or keeping the answer on the track. The remembering is there too, but that drifting when I’m trying to answer is what worries me more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders off because you're not interested in what you're doing, or because you're having trouble keeping your thoughts organized?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because I’m not interested. I mean, at the shop I am trying to answer them, and I care about getting it right.\n\nIt feels more like I can’t keep the thoughts in order. Someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start there, but then I’m on the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the garden soil by the shed, and then mortgage papers somehow come into it. And I can see the person’s face like… they’re losing the thread because I lost it first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on topic is related to a change in your thinking or memory, or is it more about being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like my thinking gets… tangled, not just distracted. I came in because people ask me a simple thing, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I start to answer that, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil is in my head, and then the mortgage papers.\n\nAnd I can see their face, like I’ve lost them. Or I’ve lost myself first, maybe. It’s not that I don’t care about the answer. It just doesn’t stay in the right lane.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on topic and answering questions are affecting your daily life more than your feelings of sadness or embarrassment?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is affecting things more, or at least it’s the part that makes trouble in the day.\n\nThe sadness is there, and I get embarrassed—especially when a customer is looking at me and I know I’m not making sense—but the staying on topic part is what’s made me cut back on custom work. I used to handle those conversations fine, all the details about settings and sizes and dates, and now I lose track during consultations.\n\nAnd then repairs… I miss deadlines because I think I’ve said one thing or written it down, and then the invoice—my husband has been checking those for me now. I used to do that myself. It’s humiliating, but also practical, because if the numbers are wrong, or the date is wrong, then the whole thing gets messy."
